**You:**

Please do an analysis of what we are doing in this project with python.
Can Claude accomplish our pipeline without needing python? Have we not built something better, have we wasted time?

* * *

**ChatGPT:**

Verdict

You did not waste the project. You did, however, build more orchestration than the current results justify.

The useful core is real. The project now has deterministic task contracts, graph validation, source-identity checks, write-boundary enforcement, evidence verification, decomposition validation, and independent model review. That is substantially safer and more auditable than simply telling Claude, “pick a task and finish it.”

But we have also built a fairly large custom agent-management system before proving that it completes real game features faster than a simpler Claude-native workflow.

My blunt assessment is:

Claude can replace much of our orchestration Python.
Claude cannot replace the deterministic authority without equivalent non-LLM code somewhere.
We built something better for control and auditability.
We have not yet shown that it is better for development speed or finished-game output.
The correct response is simplification, not deletion.
What we have actually built

The project is better described today as an agent verification and governance platform than as a complete autonomous game-production pipeline.

The conceptual flow is:

The GDD defines intent.
TaskGraph turns intent into structured contracts, dependencies, resource locks, acceptance criteria, validation requirements, and completion evidence.
Deterministic Python checks exact facts.
Claude and Codex perform semantic work: decomposition, implementation, test creation, review, and repair.
Git identity, file boundaries, tests, evidence hashes, and graph consistency are verified externally.
A human still authorizes application, delivery, and merging.

That division is explicitly part of the repository design: deterministic tools establish facts and graph state; language models make bounded semantic judgments; humans retain ambiguity-resolution and application authority. The same design document also acknowledges that generic D1C graph application, dependency readiness, autonomous dispatch, automatic merging, and GDDRAG-assisted decomposition are not implemented yet.

A useful analogy is:

NoSafeCircle concept	Conventional software equivalent
Task contracts	Typed interfaces
TaskGraph	Build/dependency graph
Claude/Codex output	Untrusted generated code
Deterministic validators	Compiler/type checker
Evidence artifacts	Build provenance
Human application/merge	Release gate

That is not pointless infrastructure. It solves a real problem: an agent can produce plausible text saying it complied without actually complying.

Could Claude accomplish the pipeline without Python?
Claude could accomplish most of the work

Claude Code now provides a lot of functionality that overlaps with our custom implementation:

Headless execution with bounded turns, tool controls, machine-readable output, and schema-constrained structured results.
Subagents with separate contexts, models, tools, permission modes, hooks, and worktree isolation. Claude (https://code.claude.com/docs/en/sub-agents)
Hooks that can inspect or block tool use, prevent completion, run commands, and respond to subagent or worktree events. Claude (https://code.claude.com/docs/en/hooks)
An external permission system in which deny rules take precedence and runtime hooks can make enforcement decisions. Importantly, these permissions are enforced outside the model rather than merely entrusted to the model. Claude (https://code.claude.com/docs/en/permissions)

Therefore, Claude could directly handle:

Task selection
Repository exploration
Semantic decomposition
Implementer/test-author/validator roles
Worktree creation
Most role sequencing
Basic retries
Tool permissions
Structured JSON generation
Running Unity and Git commands
Preparing commits and pull requests

So yes: we could create a much smaller system where Claude orchestrates most of the workflow directly.

Claude cannot provide the same guarantees by itself

There is a critical distinction between:

“Can we do this without Python?”

and:

“Can we do this without deterministic external enforcement?”

The first answer is yes. The second answer is no, assuming we still want the same guarantees.

A model should not be the only entity deciding all three of these:

What it was allowed to do.
Whether it obeyed the rules.
Whether the resulting task is complete.

For example, Claude can be instructed not to write outside Assets/NoSafeCircle/RangedEnemy/. But an independent process should still inspect the actual Git diff and fail if another file changed. Claude can generate a graph delta, but exact acceptance-criteria coverage and cycle detection should not depend on Claude’s opinion. Claude can report that tests passed, but the test process and captured result must remain authoritative.

Removing Python entirely would therefore mean one of three things:

Move the validators into TypeScript, Rust, C#, shell scripts, GitHub Actions, or Claude hooks.
Rely on Anthropic’s deterministic runtime for more of the enforcement.
Abandon some guarantees and trust model self-reporting.

Option 1 changes the language but not the architecture. Option 2 reduces our maintenance burden but increases reliance on Claude-specific infrastructure. Option 3 is a genuine regression.

The valuable invention is not Python. It is the boundary between model judgment and deterministic authority.

What we genuinely built better
TaskGraph is real infrastructure

TaskGraph is not merely a wrapper around a prompt. It separates approved task definitions from mutable completion claims, validates the whole dependency graph, handles evidence records, checks Git identities and hashes, and performs atomic migrations. It also deliberately keeps graph proposals separate from graph application.

Claude could write a task YAML file. It should not be solely responsible for determining whether:

every parent criterion is covered exactly,
dependencies exist and remain acyclic,
task identifiers are unique,
resource locks are valid,
evidence points to the correct commit and tree,
a migration is atomic,
a claimed completion remains conformant.

That domain model is worth keeping.

The decomposition validation is more than “ask two models”

D1A validates exact acceptance, validation, and integration coverage; traceability; graph topology; resource groups; and the proposed full graph. D1B.2 separates candidate authorship from independent review and revalidates every candidate deterministically.

The round-robin implementation also preserves source identity, requires distinct providers, constrains tools and write access, records canonical hashes, and produces immutable artifacts.

A Claude-only workflow could reproduce the semantic conversation. It would not reproduce provider independence: three Claude subagents remain three instances from the same model family. Whether Codex-versus-Claude independence is worth its cost still needs measurement, but it is a real capability rather than empty ceremony.

Execution isolation is valuable

ExecutionCrew verifies the source baseline, limits read and write scopes, uses disposable workspaces, inspects Git changes after each role, checks patch applicability, permits only a bounded repair, and leaves delivery claims to a separate process.

That is materially safer than depending on a long prompt telling an agent to “please stay within scope.”

The immutable evidence model is valuable

AgentRuntime normalizes provider failures, validates structured results, enforces path and execution budgets, records exact models and limits, cleans up process groups, and creates immutable run artifacts. It also intentionally prevents provider adapters from declaring task delivery or conformance.

Some of that can now be delegated to Claude Code or the Agent SDK. The underlying rule—that model output is evidence, not authority—is still correct.

Where we overbuilt

This is the part I would not sugarcoat.

1. Our custom orchestration layer is too large

There are at least 100 Python files under Pipeline/, including many smoke-test modules. The search result was capped, so the precise total may be higher.

Pipeline/ExecutionCrew/run_crew.py alone is approximately 92 KB, with another approximately 18 KB of prompts. Meanwhile, the generic TaskExecution layer is only a few kilobytes.

That suggests that too much policy, sequencing, process management, and state handling has accumulated in one implementation crew. It may all have a reason, but the burden of proof has now reversed: each custom behavior should justify why Claude’s native subagents, worktrees, hooks, structured outputs, and permissions cannot perform it.

My engineering estimate—not a measured result—is that roughly one-third to one-half of the orchestration code could probably be retired or substantially flattened, while preserving the important TaskGraph and validation logic.

2. We polished review more than application

The design explicitly says D1C generic graph application is still missing. Consequently, decomposition can be carefully authored, independently reviewed, hash-checked, graph-validated, and packaged—and then still require a custom or manual application step.

The recent NSC-016 run illustrates this well: two model calls produced a validated three-child decomposition in roughly 369 seconds, ending in review_ready with the source unchanged. That is strong evidence of integrity, but it is not yet evidence of end-to-end productivity. 

We have built an excellent airport-security checkpoint while part of the road from the airport to the destination is still manual.

3. Enforcement at the repository boundary is weaker than the internal machinery

The repository currently has a narrow deterministic workflow focused on D1B.2-related paths and smoke suites. It does not appear to provide equivalent required checks across every pipeline subsystem.

The current main branch is also reported by GitHub as unprotected.

That means we have elaborate internal rules, but GitHub does not yet force every merge to obey them. A deterministic rule that can be bypassed at the final repository boundary is still partly a convention.

Before adding more agents, we should make the important existing checks mandatory.

4. Native Claude capabilities now duplicate some earlier work

Some of our runtime code was reasonable when built, but Claude Code has moved toward the same architecture:

External permission enforcement
Hook-based validation
Isolated worktrees
Subagent roles
Bounded headless execution
Structured schema output

This does not mean our original decisions were foolish. It means the product landscape changed and we should stop paying to maintain functions that are now commodity capabilities.

Relying on Claude Code here does not mean “trusting Claude.” It means relying on Anthropic’s deterministic runtime for generic mechanics while keeping our game-specific invariants under our control.

5. We have not run the benchmark that would justify the complexity

We currently cannot honestly say that the full pipeline is faster, cheaper, or produces fewer escaped defects than:

Claude Code in a worktree, using a concise task contract, native subagents, and a small set of deterministic validators.

We believe the additional review catches defects. It probably does. But without comparative task data, we do not know whether it catches enough defects to repay:

model-call latency,
context packaging,
artifact-review time,
runtime maintenance,
failed-provider recovery,
additional handoffs,
and the cognitive burden of understanding the pipeline itself.

This is the largest unanswered question.

What I would keep, shrink, and stop
Area	Decision
TaskGraph schema and full-graph validation	Keep
Exact D1A coverage and graph-delta validation	Keep
Git source identity and write-scope checking	Keep
Evidence hashes, test-result parsing, and conformance	Keep
Atomic graph application and rollback	Build next
Claude/Codex independent review	Keep as escalation until measured
Custom structured-output parsing and generic retries	Replace where native support passes our tests
Basic role sequencing	Move toward Claude subagents
Custom worktree/process setup	Prefer native mechanisms where adequate
Giant ExecutionCrew state machine	Break apart and reduce
More new agent frameworks	Stop for now
GDDRAG/autonomous dispatch/auto-merge	Defer until the shorter loop proves itself
The architecture we should move toward
Claude-native coordination

Claude should become responsible for the semantic and procedural flow:

Read the task contract.
Create the appropriate worktree.
Delegate implementation, testing, and review to bounded subagents.
Run repository commands.
Request deterministic checks through hooks or CLI commands.
Repair failures within an explicit budget.
Produce a delivery candidate.
A thin deterministic NoSafeCircle kernel

Python should become a small collection of authoritative, mostly pure commands:

nsc task select
nsc task validate
nsc graph validate
nsc graph apply --plan <artifact>
nsc diff verify --task NSC-???
nsc evidence package --task NSC-???
nsc evidence verify --task NSC-???
nsc conformance check --task NSC-???

These commands should accept explicit inputs, return structured results, and avoid knowing whether Claude, Codex, a human, or CI invoked them.

That would preserve what is genuinely special about the project while outsourcing generic agent mechanics.

Mandatory CI and branch protection

The same kernel commands should run in GitHub Actions. Required checks should include:

Graph validity
Contract validity
Evidence validity
Unauthorized scope changes
Python tests
Unity edit-mode/play-mode tests where applicable
Generated artifact consistency

Then protect main.

That turns our governance from “the recommended path” into “the actual repository law.”

The experiment that should decide this

Freeze new infrastructure and run approximately 6–10 representative real game tasks using two paths:

Path A: current pipeline

Use TaskGraph, current runtime, ExecutionCrew, decomposition round-robin where required, delivery, and existing review steps.

Path B: simplified workflow

Use Claude Code native subagents, hooks, permissions, worktrees, and structured output, calling only the thin deterministic validators.

Measure:

Measurement	Why it matters
Wall-clock time to accepted feature	Actual development speed
Human interventions	Whether automation is really helping
Model cost and token use	Ongoing operating cost
Out-of-scope modifications	Guardrail effectiveness
Invalid contracts or graph changes	Deterministic correctness
Defects found during review	Value of extra reviewer stages
Defects escaping into Unity validation	Real quality
Time spent reviewing artifacts	Hidden human cost
Recovery after interruption/failure	Operational resilience
Lines/modules required to support the run	Maintenance burden

A layer stays only when it demonstrably catches important failures, lowers human effort, or makes recovery materially better. “We already built it” is not a reason to retain it.

Final answer

No, you did not waste your time.

You correctly recognized that agents need external contracts, deterministic validation, evidence, and bounded authority. Claude’s own architecture now uses external permissions, hooks, schemas, and isolated workspaces, which supports the central idea rather than disproving it. Claude+1 (https://code.claude.com/docs/en/hooks)

But we are now at the point where continuing to add Python would become wasteful. We have enough infrastructure to test the thesis. We do not need another layer before doing so.

The strongest description is:

We built a valuable deterministic core surrounded by an orchestration shell that has become too large.

Keep the contracts, graph rules, evidence, Git verification, and conformance checks. Let Claude take over more role coordination, structured interaction, worktrees, and retries. Finish generic graph application and mandatory CI before building anything more ambitious.

Keep the constitution. Cut the bureaucracy.

**You:**

So what I would like to do is get through a game task and reach testing phase instead of stumbling over errors, I would like to not need to keep doing that. What work flow could we use so I can put in the prompt once into my powershell, and eventually I will be at the testing phase. What do we need?

**ChatGPT:**

Yes. We should build a Task-to-Test workflow

The goal should not be “automatically finish and deliver a task.” That is too broad for the first reliable version.

The goal should be one precise terminal state:

READY_FOR_HUMAN_UNITY_VALIDATION

At that point:

one appropriate game task has been selected and claimed;
its isolated task checkout and branch exist;
implementation and automated tests have been authored;
the generated candidate has passed deterministic scope checks and semantic review;
the candidate has been applied only to the isolated task branch;
a local checkpoint commit exists;
targeted Unity tests have run;
ordinary compile/test failures have received bounded automatic repair attempts;
the repository is clean;
you receive the task checkout path, test results, and a manual runtime-validation checklist;
nothing has been pushed, merged, delivered, or declared conformant.

That is the useful point where you open Unity and evaluate the actual game feature instead of debugging the pipeline.

Why the current workflow cannot do this continuously

The individual pieces mostly exist, but nothing currently conducts them from beginning to end.

Task selection is still partly subjective

taskcontrol states --state not_delivered discovers unfinished work, but the repository explicitly says this does not establish dependency readiness. A model still has to inspect dependencies and decide whether a task can actually start.

Checkout creation is not resumable

task_checkout.py checkout handles a specifically selected task and creates a clone, but it stops when the destination already exists. It does not reconcile or resume an interrupted task checkout. It also only generates Issue and claim text; it does not actually perform the GitHub claim.

There is also path-convention drift. The mandatory orchestration document says the canonical task checkout is:

C:\UnityProjects\NoSafeCircleAgentCrew\<TASK-ID>

but the helper’s internal default still constructs the older NoSafeCircle-NSC... style, and another quick-start document still demonstrates that older naming.

ExecutionCrew requires manual path planning

Before ExecutionCrew can start, somebody must correctly choose every:

existing implementation path;
new implementation path;
existing test path;
new test path.

The CLI rejects a normal run without an explicit implementation surface and test surface. That is a sound guardrail, but it is currently a recurring manual setup hazard.

ExecutionCrew deliberately stops before testing

ExecutionCrew can run:

Contract Locality Auditor
→ Implementer
→ Test Author
→ Validator

and produce candidate.patch, but review_ready is still review-only. It does not apply the patch to the task checkout.

This is the biggest missing bridge.

The clean Unity runner requires a committed, clean candidate

The existing Unity test wrapper is strong. It verifies test results and detects repository mutations. But it requires the checkout to begin clean, and it requires an explicit test platform and test filter.

So the current pipeline is effectively:

generate candidate
→ stop
→ human finds patch
→ human checks patch
→ human applies patch
→ human resolves workspace state
→ human determines test commands
→ human runs tests
→ human carries failures back into another agent run

That is exactly the clerical gap causing you to stumble.

The workflow we should use

Use Claude Code as the outer supervisor and retain the existing deterministic Python tools as the authority kernel.

Claude Code supports noninteractive execution through -p, structured output, bounded turns, explicit tool access, and scripted operation. Claude Code hooks can independently block commands even when other tool permissions allow them. Claude+1 (https://code.claude.com/docs/en/headless?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

The outer supervisor should run this state machine:

ONE POWERSHELL REQUEST
        ↓
Controller preflight
        ↓
Select and claim one implementation-ready task
        ↓
Create or resume canonical task checkout
        ↓
Generate and validate exact execution-path plan
        ↓
Run ExecutionCrew
        ↓
review_ready candidate?
        ↓ yes
Deterministically apply candidate to task branch
        ↓
Create local candidate checkpoint commit
        ↓
Run targeted Unity tests
        ↓
pass? ──────────────── yes ─→ READY_FOR_HUMAN_UNITY_VALIDATION
  │
  no
  ↓
Convert compiler/test failures into bounded repair feedback
        ↓
ExecutionCrew --retry-run
        ↓
Apply corrected candidate
        ↓
Recommit and retest
        ↓
repeat within fixed budget
1. Controller preflight

The supervisor should use either the canonical clean main checkout or, preferably, a dedicated controller checkout that is never used for gameplay edits.

It checks all predictable environmental failures before claiming a task:

git available
python available
docker available
docker compose available
claude authenticated
provider Docker volumes authenticated
gh authenticated
Unity executable discoverable
origin/main reachable
TaskGraph valid
controller checkout clean

This catches authentication, Compose-volume, Git, and Unity-location problems before a task is claimed.

Every Docker invocation must use:

-p nosafecircle

because the repository documents that clone-derived Compose project names can otherwise create empty provider-auth volumes and cause authentication failures.

2. Select one actually executable task

For the first production version, restrict selection to:

contract_disposition: active
kind: implementation
execution_scope: single_agent
decomposition_state: concrete
state: not_delivered
all dependencies currently conformant

That deliberately excludes decomposition work. Decomposition introduces another human application boundary and is not necessary for proving that we can take a concrete task to Unity testing.

The supervisor then checks:

exact GitHub Issue state;
assignment/claim state;
exclusive-resource conflicts;
canonical checkout collision;
whether the task is game work rather than more pipeline infrastructure.

If a generic request selected a task whose locality audit later exposes a bad contract, the supervisor releases it and tries the next viable candidate. If you explicitly name a task, it stops on that task’s blocker instead.

3. Create or resume the task checkout

The checkout stage should support three outcomes:

new checkout created
existing matching checkout resumed
existing conflicting checkout requires human reconciliation

It must never create increasingly creative duplicate directories.

Canonical form:

C:\UnityProjects\NoSafeCircleAgentCrew\NSC-###

The checkout manifest should record:

{
  "task_id": "NSC-###",
  "source_main_commit": "...",
  "task_contract_revision": 1,
  "task_contract_sha256": "...",
  "branch": "nsc-###-...",
  "checkout": "C:\\UnityProjects\\NoSafeCircleAgentCrew\\NSC-###",
  "workflow_state": "planning_execution"
}

Every later invocation reads this manifest and resumes instead of starting over.

4. Have Claude propose the paths, then validate them deterministically

We should not remove ExecutionCrew’s exact-file boundaries. We should automate their preparation.

Claude produces an execution_plan.json such as:

{
  "task_id": "NSC-050",
  "provider": "claude",
  "existing_implementation_paths": [
    "Assets/NoSafeCircle/DoorPrototype/Scripts/RangedEnemyMovement.cs"
  ],
  "new_implementation_paths": [],
  "existing_test_paths": [],
  "new_test_paths": [
    "Assets/NoSafeCircle/DoorPrototype/Tests/RangedEnemyMovementPlayModeTests.cs"
  ],
  "unity_test_runs": [
    {
      "platform": "PlayMode",
      "filter": "NoSafeCircle.DoorPrototype.Tests.RangedEnemyMovementPlayModeTests"
    }
  ],
  "reasoning_summary": "..."
}

A small deterministic validator then confirms:

the task identity and contract hash;
existing paths really are tracked regular files;
new paths really are absent and untracked;
parent directories already exist as committed Git trees;
no .meta path was requested;
implementation and test scopes are disjoint;
no path escapes the repository;
no task contract, GDD, pipeline file, or unrelated scene is included;
test filters are nonempty and associated with the approved test files.

An invalid plan goes back to Claude with exact findings. It does not become another PowerShell problem for you.

5. Run ExecutionCrew normally

Once the plan validates, the supervisor translates it into the existing ExecutionCrew command.

The supervisor handles routine outcomes:

transient provider failure: retry once or use the approved alternate provider;
invalid proposed scope: revise the execution plan;
already-claimed task: select another candidate;
CONTRACT_REVIEW_REQUIRED: release a generically selected candidate and try another;
ordinary needs_changes: let ExecutionCrew use its existing repair cycle;
review_ready: continue automatically;
hard design ambiguity or exhausted repair budget: stop with preserved diagnostics.

No task hopping occurs merely because code compilation or tests are difficult. Those belong inside the task’s repair loop.

6. Add the missing apply-for-testing bridge

This is the highest-value new deterministic tool.

Proposed command:

python Pipeline/Supervisor/apply_candidate_for_testing.py --run <RUN-DIRECTORY> --checkout C:\UnityProjects\NoSafeCircleAgentCrew\NSC-###

It should require all of the following:

the checkout branch is not main;
the checkout is clean;
crew_result.json says review_ready;
the task ID, contract identity, source commit, and patch hash match;
git apply --check succeeds;
the candidate’s actual path set exactly matches the approved ExecutionCrew scope;
TaskGraph still validates after application;
git diff --check succeeds.

Then it applies the patch and creates a local checkpoint commit such as:

test-candidate(NSC-050): ranged enemy keep-away locomotion

That commit is not a delivery or approval claim. Its purposes are:

create a clean testable checkout;
make rollback deterministic;
give the Unity test manifest an exact commit/tree;
allow the workflow to resume safely;
prevent Unity test churn from being confused with candidate edits.

The initial PowerShell request should explicitly authorize this local task-branch commit. It should not authorize pushing, merging, or changing main.

7. Run tests and feed failures back automatically

The supervisor runs the test entries from the validated execution plan using the existing clean Unity test wrapper.

On success, it preserves:

validation manifest;
XML;
Unity log;
tested commit and tree;
test counts.

On failure, it should classify the result:

compiler failure
test assertion failure
Unity process failure
missing/malformed result
unexpected repository mutation

For compiler and assertion failures, it creates a bounded feedback file containing the relevant errors and calls the existing ExecutionCrew retry mechanism:

docker compose -p nosafecircle run --rm -T claude-exec python3 Pipeline/ExecutionCrew/run_crew.py --retry-run <PRIOR-RUN-ID> --review-feedback-file <FEEDBACK-FILE>

ExecutionCrew already knows how to inherit the original task, provider, implementation paths, test paths, and prior candidate during a human-review retry.

The new supervisor would reuse that mechanism for test-driven repairs:

test failed
→ summarize deterministic failures
→ retry prior candidate
→ receive corrected review_ready candidate
→ apply/checkpoint
→ rerun test

Use a fixed repair budget, probably two post-Unity repair rounds. An exhausted budget stops with all logs and patches preserved.

If Unity mutates the repository, the supervisor first archives the diagnostic diff. Because testing began from a known clean candidate checkpoint in an isolated task clone, it can then restore that checkpoint before attempting a repair. It must never hide the mutation without preserving it.

8. Stop at the useful human handoff

Successful output should be simple:

READY FOR HUMAN UNITY VALIDATION

Task:       NSC-050 — Ranged Enemy Keep-Away Locomotion
Checkout:   C:\UnityProjects\NoSafeCircleAgentCrew\NSC-050
Branch:     nsc-050-ranged-enemy-keep-away
HEAD:       abc123...
Automated:
  EditMode: PASSED
  PlayMode: PASSED

Open:
  C:\UnityProjects\NoSafeCircleAgentCrew\NSC-050

Manual validation:
  1. Open Assets/Scenes/DoorPrototype.unity.
  2. Confirm the ranged enemy advances when outside its preferred range.
  3. Confirm it retreats when the player enters its minimum range.
  4. Confirm Frost slows repositioning without changing attack timing.

Not performed:
  push
  merge
  delivery evidence
  conformance claim

That is where your attention becomes valuable again.

What we need to build
Required for the first explicit-task MVP
Execution-plan schema and validator Converts Claude’s proposed files and test filters into safely validated ExecutionCrew arguments.
Apply-for-testing bridge Verifies and applies only a review_ready candidate to the isolated task branch, then creates the clean candidate checkpoint.
Unity test-and-repair coordinator Runs the existing clean tests, extracts useful failures, invokes ExecutionCrew --retry-run, and retests within a bounded budget.
Resumable task-to-test state file Records each completed phase so rerunning the command resumes rather than recreating the checkout or restarting ExecutionCrew.
One PowerShell launcher Performs tool/authentication preflight and starts Claude Code in noninteractive supervisor mode.
Required for fully generic “pick one for me” mode
Implementation-ready candidate command Something equivalent to:
python Pipeline/TaskGraph/taskcontrol.py candidates --implementation-ready --json
It should deterministically calculate the contract/state/dependency portion of candidate eligibility. GitHub claims and resource conflicts remain live operational checks.
GitHub claim automation Use authenticated gh commands to create/find the Issue, assign it, post the planned approach, release it on a hard generic-candidate blocker, and post the testing handoff.
Checkout resume/reconciliation Extend task_checkout.py so a matching canonical checkout resumes safely instead of simply failing because the directory exists.
Claude Code safety settings and hooks The supervisor needs Bash and repository access, but hooks should deny:
git push
merging into main
direct edits in the controller/main checkout
destructive reset/clean outside the isolated task checkout
task-contract or GDD edits during task-to-test mode
evidence/conformance publication
The final user experience

After this exists, your normal command should be one line:

.\Pipeline\Supervisor\Start-GameTaskToTest.ps1 -Request "Pick the best available game implementation task and take it to human Unity testing."

The launcher—not you—supplies the detailed fixed authorization:

May:
- select and claim one suitable implementation task;
- create or resume its isolated canonical checkout;
- run ExecutionCrew;
- apply only a review_ready candidate inside that checkout;
- create local task-branch checkpoint commits;
- run Unity tests;
- repair compile/test failures within the original scope.

May not:
- alter main;
- push or merge;
- widen the approved file scope silently;
- edit the GDD or task contracts;
- package delivery evidence;
- declare delivery or conformance.

The proposed launcher does not exist yet. It is the next thing worth building.

Recommended implementation order

Do not attempt generic task selection, decomposition, delivery, and automatic merging all at once.

Build this in three bounded stages:

Stage 1 — Explicit task to automated tests

Input:

.\Pipeline\Supervisor\Start-GameTaskToTest.ps1 -TaskId NSC-### 

Automate:

path planning
→ ExecutionCrew
→ candidate application
→ checkpoint commit
→ Unity tests
→ bounded repair
→ testing handoff

This proves the difficult center of the workflow.

Stage 2 — Generic selection and GitHub claim

Add:

-Request "Pick the best available game task..."

with deterministic dependency-ready candidates, live Issue/resource checks, canonical checkout creation, and resume behavior.

Stage 3 — Hardened Claude supervisor

Add the stored supervisor prompt, Claude Code hooks, structured progress, interruption recovery, and one-line user command.

We do not need GDDRAG, autonomous merge, automatic delivery evidence, or a replacement for ExecutionCrew to solve your immediate problem.

We need to connect the tools we already have:

select → plan scope → execute → apply → checkpoint → test → repair → hand off.

That should be the next pipeline project.

**You:**

I have a different idea. Why dont we create a goal oriented open ai agent, with the purpose of using my pipeline to get to human review on a task?

* * *

**ChatGPT:**

Yes. This is the better design.

Instead of hard-coding one giant “Stage 1” script that must anticipate every failure, we should create a goal-oriented OpenAI supervisor whose job is:

Given an explicit NSC-### implementation task, use the existing No Safe Circle pipeline until that task has a deterministically verified review_ready candidate for human review—or stop only at a genuine human-authority boundary.

That directly attacks the problem you described: you should not have to keep interpreting paths, rerunning commands, finding artifacts, and figuring out which pipeline error means “retry,” “repair,” or “stop.”

What “goal-oriented” means here

We reuse the strongest idea from Assignment 5:

Desired State - Current State = Remaining Gap

Then:

Observe current state
→ identify the next unmet condition
→ choose one approved action
→ execute it
→ observe again
→ continue until the goal is proven

Your old Assignment 5 agent used that pattern to compare the GDD against Assets/ and choose a feature. The new production agent would use the same reasoning pattern, but its desired state would be an exact pipeline artifact rather than a broad gameplay feature.

The exact terminal condition

The agent is allowed to return:

HUMAN_REVIEW_READY

only when deterministic code proves all of the following:

Task contract is still the expected revision/hash
Task is active + implementation + single_agent + concrete
Task checkout exists on the correct feature branch
Source identity has not changed unexpectedly
ExecutionCrew finished with crew_status=review_ready
candidate.patch exists
candidate.patch hash matches crew_result.json
candidate.patch passes git apply --check
The authoritative source checkout remains unchanged

The model cannot simply say, “It looks ready.”

A final output guardrail checks the actual run artifacts before allowing the success response.

The handoff should look like this:

HUMAN REVIEW READY

Task:          NSC-050
Checkout:      C:\UnityProjects\NoSafeCircleAgentCrew\NSC-050
Branch:        nsc-050-door-lock-break-state
Source HEAD:   5ee2272...
Crew run:      nsc-050-...
Candidate:     C:\...\candidate.patch
Crew status:   review_ready

Review commands:
  git apply --check <candidate>
  git apply --stat <candidate>

Not performed:
  patch application
  Unity testing
  implementation commit
  push
  merge
  delivery evidence
  conformance claim

That is a real, narrow, verifiable goal.

The correct architecture
PowerShell command
        ↓
OpenAI Task Review Supervisor
        ↓
Observe current pipeline state
        ↓
Choose from approved typed tools
        ↓
TaskGraph / Git / Checkout / ExecutionCrew
        ↓
Re-observe results
        ↓
Continue, retry, replan, or stop
        ↓
HUMAN_REVIEW_READY

The OpenAI agent handles judgment and recovery.

Your existing Python pipeline continues enforcing facts and authority.

That is the right division of labor.

Use the OpenAI Agents SDK for the outer loop

The OpenAI Agents SDK is a good fit because it already provides:

a tool-using agent loop that continues until the task is complete;
typed function tools with Pydantic-generated schemas;
input, output, and tool guardrails;
persistent sessions;
structured final output;
tracing of model calls and tool calls;
bounded turns and recoverable tool errors. OpenAI GitHub+1 (https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/tools/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

A persistent SQLite session can preserve the agent conversation and tool history across invocations. We should also retain a separate deterministic goal_state.json, because model conversation history must not become workflow authority. OpenAI GitHub (https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/sessions/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

The SDK route requires an OPENAI_API_KEY, so the launcher must preflight that credential before creating or claiming a task. OpenAI GitHub (https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

One manager agent, not another crew

We do not need another hierarchy of planner, implementer, reviewer, and validator agents.

ExecutionCrew already contains:

Contract Locality Auditor
→ Implementer
→ Test Author
→ Validator
→ bounded repair

The new OpenAI agent sits above that system.

Its responsibility is only:

prepare
→ invoke
→ inspect
→ recover
→ reach the human boundary

That prevents us from rebuilding the same roles twice.

The agent’s approved tools

The manager should receive only custom function tools. It should not receive unrestricted shell, arbitrary file-writing, or patch-application tools.

Custom function tools can be schema-validated and wrapped in input/output guardrails before and after execution. OpenAI GitHub (https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/ref/tool_guardrails/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

Read-only observation tools
observe_environment

Returns structured facts:

{
  "git_available": true,
  "docker_available": true,
  "compose_available": true,
  "github_available": true,
  "openai_api_key_available": true,
  "provider_auth_available": {
    "claude": true,
    "codex": true
  },
  "controller_clean": true,
  "origin_main": "..."
}
observe_task

Runs the existing TaskGraph commands and returns:

contract identity;
disposition;
kind;
execution scope;
decomposition state;
evidence-derived state;
dependencies and their states;
exclusive resources;
acceptance criteria;
validation gates.
observe_checkout

Returns:

whether the canonical checkout exists;
remote;
branch;
HEAD/tree;
cleanliness;
whether it belongs to the requested task;
previous Task Review Agent state;
previous ExecutionCrew runs.
read_repository_file

Allows reading only approved repository-relative text files in the isolated task checkout.

search_repository

Allows bounded read-only search in:

Assets/
Packages/
ProjectSettings/
Tasks/<selected-task>.yaml
Docs/GDD/No_Safe_Circle_GDD.md
Docs/Engineering/UNITY_TESTING_POLICY.md

It does not expose credentials, .git, outputs from other task runs, or unrestricted filesystem paths.

Controlled side-effect tools
prepare_task_checkout

Creates or safely resumes:

C:\UnityProjects\NoSafeCircleAgentCrew\<TASK-ID>

It never invents duplicate -NEW, -FINAL, or timestamped checkout names.

Possible results:

created
resumed
conflict_requires_human
check_and_claim_task

Checks the GitHub Issue and resource ownership, then claims the task when available.

It cannot close the Issue, merge a pull request, or mark the task delivered.

validate_execution_scope

The agent proposes:

{
  "existing_implementation_paths": [],
  "new_implementation_paths": [],
  "existing_test_paths": [],
  "new_test_paths": []
}

The deterministic tool checks:

tracked-versus-new path classification;
allowed repository locations;
parent directory existence;
implementation/test separation;
.meta exclusions;
scene/resource ownership;
task contract identity;
no pipeline, GDD, TaskGraph, or unrelated task edits.

The model can correct a rejected scope plan and try again.

run_execution_crew

Receives only a validated scope-plan ID—not arbitrary shell arguments.

Internally it constructs the exact Docker Compose command:

docker compose -p nosafecircle run --rm -T <provider>-exec ...

It returns structured results and host paths.

inspect_execution_run

Parses:

crew_result.json;
locality audit;
role results;
deterministic rejection reasons;
candidate or diagnostic artifacts;
provider failure classification;
recommended recoverability.
verify_human_review_ready

This is the only tool allowed to mint a success proof.

It returns a signed/hash-bound structured observation showing that the candidate really meets the terminal condition.

What the agent may adapt to

This is where the goal-oriented agent earns its keep.

It should automatically handle routine problems such as:

Observed problem	Agent response
Proposed existing path is actually absent	Reclassify as new_*_path
Proposed new path is already tracked	Reclassify as existing
Test file guess was wrong	Search current tests and revise the plan
Parent directory does not exist	Choose an existing committed test directory
GitHub Issue already claimed	Stop for explicit task; select another only in later generic mode
Checkout already exists and matches	Resume it
Checkout exists but conflicts	Stop with exact reconciliation information
Transient provider failure	Retry within budget or use configured fallback
Invalid model JSON	Retry with the schema error
ExecutionCrew says needs_changes	Let its built-in repair cycle operate
ExecutionCrew rejects an invalid scope	Inspect, revise, rerun
Locality audit says contract review required	Stop at human authority
Design is ambiguous	Stop at human authority
Candidate reaches review_ready	Verify artifacts and finish

The agent must not “solve” a contract defect by editing the contract or GDD.

What it must never do

For the first version, there should be no tools for:

direct gameplay implementation
direct test implementation
arbitrary shell execution
applying candidate.patch
opening or running Unity
committing implementation
pushing
merging
editing main
editing Tasks/*.yaml
editing the GDD
packaging delivery evidence
claiming conformance
decomposition
automatic task substitution

ExecutionCrew performs the code generation. The new agent gets us reliably to the candidate review boundary.

Once that works, we can add two new goal actions:

apply reviewed candidate
run Unity tests

and change the terminal condition to:

READY_FOR_HUMAN_UNITY_VALIDATION

The architecture remains the same.

MVP acceptance criteria

The first production slice is done when all of these are true:

One PowerShell command accepts an explicit NSC-###.
The agent validates task eligibility and dependencies.
It creates or resumes the canonical checkout.
It determines exact implementation and test paths through read-only inspection.
Invalid path plans are rejected deterministically and can be corrected by the agent.
It launches ExecutionCrew without asking you to compose the command.
It interprets normal failures and retries within a fixed budget.
It can stop cleanly on CONTRACT_REVIEW_REQUIRED.
It can prove and report HUMAN_REVIEW_READY.
A fake-provider smoke suite proves each major branch.
It cannot edit code, apply the candidate, commit, push, merge, or alter authority files.
An interrupted run can resume from the same checkout and session.

First implementation milestone

The first commit should not call the real API or ExecutionCrew yet.

Build a vertical slice using fake tools:

explicit task request
→ fake observation
→ agent recognizes missing checkout
→ fake prepare checkout
→ agent recognizes missing scope
→ fake scope validation rejects first plan
→ agent revises plan
→ fake ExecutionCrew returns review_ready
→ final output guardrail verifies proof
→ HUMAN_REVIEW_READY

That demonstrates the behavior we actually care about before granting real side effects.

Then replace the fake tools one at a time:

real TaskGraph observation
real checkout preparation
real repository read/search
real scope validation
real ExecutionCrew launch
real artifact verification

This is a much better use of a small amount of Python: Python defines safe actions and verifies outcomes; the OpenAI agent decides how to move from the current state to the goal.

What is real now
GitHub coordination inspection

For an otherwise eligible task, the agent uses authenticated gh calls to find the exact task Issue and classify it as:

available_missing
available_unassigned
claimed_by_worker
claimed_by_other
closed
conflict
unavailable

A task counts as claimed_by_worker only when:

the Issue is open;
it is assigned to cathode26;
its latest TaskReviewAgent claim comment contains the claim marker;
that comment names the exact worker ID supplied to the agent.

This stage is intentionally read-only. It cannot create, assign, comment on, close, or release the Issue yet.

Safe canonical checkout creation

The manager may create or resume only:

C:\UnityProjects\NoSafeCircleAgentCrew\<TASK-ID>

It requires:

a clean controller checkout;
controller HEAD == origin/main;
an active, concrete, single_agent implementation task;
not_delivered state;
all declared dependencies conformant;
the exact GitHub Issue already claimed by the same worker;
the canonical GitHub remote;
the exact observed task-contract hash.

The clone is first built in a temporary sibling directory. It validates the new branch, commit, tree, task contract, TaskGraph, remote, and working-tree cleanliness before renaming the clone into the canonical task path. A failed clone or validation never leaves a half-built canonical checkout.

Existing checkout handling

The new code does not delete, reset, overwrite, or create a differently named replacement for an existing task directory.

It classifies existing state as:

ready
unmanaged_exact
conflict
missing

A clean exact checkout can be adopted or resumed. A wrong branch, wrong commit, changed task contract, wrong remote, conflicting worker manifest, or dirty working tree becomes conflict and stops for human reconciliation. The identity manifest lives outside the task repository, so it does not dirty the Unity checkout.

OpenAI checkout mode

The OpenAI supervisor now has a real checkout-stage mode:

openai-checkout-real

It receives only two tools:

observe_goal_state
prepare_task_checkout

The model cannot claim an Issue, run arbitrary shell commands, edit files, choose ExecutionCrew paths, run ExecutionCrew, apply a patch, run Unity, commit, push, merge, or publish evidence.

The model must:

Observe first.
Stop at claim_task when the Issue is unavailable or unassigned.
Call checkout preparation only when deterministic state says prepare_checkout.
Re-observe after preparation.
Reach validate_scope only when the checkout is genuinely ready.

An already-ready checkout can advance without making a redundant preparation call.

**ChatGPT:**

Not exactly. You are describing too much manual handoff.

The intended workflow is:

You run one command
        ↓
Agent verifies the task and dependencies
        ↓
Agent claims the GitHub Issue under your account
        ↓
Agent creates the isolated task checkout and branch
        ↓
Agent determines the allowed implementation/test files
        ↓
Agent runs ExecutionCrew and handles ordinary retries
        ↓
Agent stops with a review-ready candidate
        ↓
You review the proposed implementation
What “assigned to you” means

The GitHub Issue will be assigned to cathode26, but that assignment is primarily a reservation marker:

This task is currently owned by Vincent’s TaskReviewAgent run. Other agents should not start it.

It does not mean you must personally write the code.

What happens with the branch

The agent should create the standalone task checkout at:

C:\UnityProjects\NoSafeCircleAgentCrew\NSC-###

and automatically create and switch it to a branch such as:

nsc-050-doorinteractable-auto-lock-durability-breaking-and-floor-reset

You should not need to manually run git checkout or git switch. You would simply open the prepared task directory when human review or Unity testing is required.

The current checkout implementation already follows this create-or-resume model and treats a wrong, dirty, or mismatched existing checkout as a conflict instead of overwriting it.

What you do at human review

You should not need to complete the task yourself.

At the first human boundary, the agent will present something like:

HUMAN REVIEW READY

Task:       NSC-###
Checkout:   C:\UnityProjects\NoSafeCircleAgentCrew\NSC-###
Branch:     nsc-###-...
Candidate:  C:\...\candidate.patch

Summary:
- Added ...
- Changed ...
- Added Play Mode tests for ...

Review needed:
- Approve candidate
- Reject with feedback

At this first boundary, the candidate is still review-only and has not been applied. That is deliberate: we want you to be able to reject a bad implementation before it alters the working task branch.

After you approve

There will probably be a second command, because approval is a real human authorization boundary. But it should be a resume, not a restart.

Conceptually:

.\Pipeline\TaskReviewAgent\Continue-TaskReviewAgent.ps1 -TaskId NSC-050 -ApproveCandidate

or perhaps the same launcher:

.\Pipeline\TaskReviewAgent\Start-TaskReviewAgent.ps1 -TaskId NSC-050 -Resume -ApproveCandidate

That continuation should:

verify the same candidate and run identity
        ↓
apply the approved patch to the existing task checkout
        ↓
create a local checkpoint commit
        ↓
run targeted Unity tests
        ↓
repair normal compiler/test failures automatically
        ↓
stop at READY_FOR_HUMAN_UNITY_VALIDATION

You would not repeat task selection, claiming, checkout creation, path planning, or the original ExecutionCrew run.

The eventual human interaction

The useful end state is approximately two commands and two meaningful human decisions:

First command
Start task NSC-###

The system works until:

HUMAN_REVIEW_READY
Your decision
Approve candidate
Resume command
Continue the same task

The system works until:

READY_FOR_HUMAN_UNITY_VALIDATION
Your decision

Open the prepared Unity project and decide whether the behavior actually feels and looks correct.

That is the right amount of human involvement. You should make design, review, and gameplay-quality decisions—not manually shepherd branches and pipeline commands.

What exists today

Currently, the real system can:

Read actual Git and TaskGraph state.
Inspect the real GitHub claim state.
Safely create or resume the canonical task checkout and branch.

The OpenAI checkout agent currently has only observation and checkout-preparation tools; it cannot yet claim the Issue, plan implementation paths, or run ExecutionCrew.

So the next work is still:

automatic GitHub claim
→ automatic implementation/test path planning
→ real ExecutionCrew invocation
→ review-ready candidate verification

After that, we build the approval/resume stage. The target is not “assign you a task and let you finish it.” The target is:

Assign and reserve the task for your agent, let the agent implement it, and involve you only where human judgment is genuinely needed.

The same issue remains open throughout. It does not get replaced by a separate “human task” issue.

GitHub remains the operational dashboard, while the task contract, Git branch, commits, test results, and TaskGraph evidence remain the durable technical authority. That fits the repository’s existing TaskGraph/GitHub split.

The issue needs three layers
1. A visible current-status section

At the top of the issue:

CURRENT STATE: HUMAN_ACTION_REQUIRED
CURRENT OWNER: Vincent
PHASE: Unity runtime validation

Task: NSC-050
Branch: nsc-050-doorinteractable-auto-lock-durability-breaking-and-floor-reset
Commit to test: 38c2197...
Checkout: C:\UnityProjects\NoSafeCircleAgentCrew\NSC-050

Next action:
Perform the Unity validation checklist below.

Do not test:
A newer commit, another checkout, or main.

This is the part you read when you return after being distracted.

2. A machine-readable workflow state

The issue body also contains a hidden managed block:

<!-- nsc-workflow-state
{
  "schema_version": "1.0",
  "task_id": "NSC-050",
  "state": "human_action_required",
  "phase": "unity_runtime_validation",
  "current_actor": "human",
  "worker_id": "task-review-agent-vincent",
  "branch": "nsc-050-doorinteractable-auto-lock-durability-breaking-and-floor-reset",
  "head_commit": "38c2197...",
  "checkout_path": "C:\\UnityProjects\\NoSafeCircleAgentCrew\\NSC-050",
  "task_contract_sha256": "...",
  "state_version": 5,
  "last_event_id": "..."
}
-->

Humans do not need to edit this JSON. Agents maintain it.

3. An append-only event log in comments

Every meaningful transition becomes a comment:

Agent implementation completed
Agent commit created
Branch pushed
Human validation requested
Human validation passed
Human validation failed
Agent repair started
Repair commit created
Delivery evidence created
Task completed

Each comment contains readable Markdown plus a hidden structured event:

<!-- nsc-workflow-event
{
  "schema_version": "1.0",
  "task_id": "NSC-050",
  "sequence": 5,
  "previous_event_id": "...",
  "event_type": "human_handoff_created",
  "from_state": "agent_working",
  "to_state": "human_action_required",
  "actor_type": "agent",
  "worker_id": "task-review-agent-vincent",
  "branch": "nsc-050-doorinteractable-auto-lock-durability-breaking-and-floor-reset",
  "head_commit": "38c2197...",
  "event_id": "..."
}
-->

The event IDs can form a hash chain. That lets the next agent detect missing, reordered, stale, or edited workflow events instead of blindly trusting the issue text.

Issue states

We do not need twenty labels. Five main states are enough:

State	Who acts next	Meaning
agent_ready	Agent	A generic agent should resume this issue
agent_working	Agent	One agent currently owns the lease
human_action_required	You	The branch is committed and waiting for your work
blocked	You or agent	A decision or external dependency prevents progress
complete	Nobody	Delivery and closeout are finished

A separate phase field tells the agent what kind of work comes next:

implementation
repair
unity_runtime_validation
delivery_evidence
merge_closeout

This is cleaner than encoding every possible phase into a separate state label.

The labels can mirror the state for fast GitHub searching:

nsc-state:agent-ready
nsc-state:agent-working
nsc-state:human-action
nsc-state:blocked
nsc-state:complete

Because all agents and you operate under the same GitHub account, the ordinary GitHub assignee cannot distinguish “the agent owns this now” from “Vincent owns this now.” The explicit workflow state solves that problem.

What the agent must do before handing the issue to you

The handoff is not allowed while the implementation exists only as an unapplied patch.

The agent must:

apply the approved candidate in the canonical task checkout
        ↓
inspect the complete diff
        ↓
run every automated check available before Unity
        ↓
commit the implementation and tests
        ↓
push the task branch
        ↓
record the exact branch and commit in the issue
        ↓
change state to HUMAN_ACTION_REQUIRED

The issue should contain:

What was implemented
- Added automatic door closing and locking after forward-side crossing.
- Added locked-door durability and public damage handling.
- Added permanent broken-door state.
- Added Player Health restoration through the existing public API.
- Added Play Mode coverage for locked-door damage and backward blocking.
What the agent already checked
- TaskGraph validation passed.
- Contract-locality audit passed.
- ExecutionCrew validator passed.
- Candidate diff scope passed.
- C# static inspection completed.
- Branch commit pushed successfully.
What you must do
1. Open:
   C:\UnityProjects\NoSafeCircleAgentCrew\NSC-050

2. Verify the current branch:
   nsc-050-doorinteractable-auto-lock-durability-breaking-and-floor-reset

3. Verify HEAD:
   38c2197...

4. Open Assets/Scenes/DoorPrototype.unity.

5. Enter Play Mode.

6. Open the door and cross forward through it.

7. Confirm the door closes and locks without a second click.

8. Confirm Player Health receives the configured recovery.

9. Attempt to walk backward through the locked doorway.

10. Apply door damage until it breaks.

11. Confirm the player still cannot move backward through the broken doorway.

12. Record PASS or FAIL in this issue.
Expected result

The issue should explicitly state what qualifies as pass and what details to report on failure.

What you do after testing

You add one human result comment.

For a pass:

## Human validation result

Result: PASS
Tested commit: 38c2197...
Unity scene: Assets/Scenes/DoorPrototype.unity

Completed steps:
- Automatic close and lock: passed
- Health restoration: passed
- Locked backward blocker: passed
- Door damage and break: passed
- Broken backward blocker: passed

Notes:
Behavior matched the requested result.

For a failure:

## Human validation result

Result: FAIL
Tested commit: 38c2197...
Unity scene: Assets/Scenes/DoorPrototype.unity

Failed step:
Door visually closed, but the player could still move backward through it.

Reproduction:
1. Open door.
2. Cross completely.
3. Wait for lock.
4. Click behind the door.
5. Player walks backward through the collider.

Expected:
Backward movement remains blocked.

Observed:
Player crossed backward.

Then you change the issue state label from:

nsc-state:human-action

to:

nsc-state:agent-ready

That is the entire resume operation. No PowerShell task ID, run ID, patch ID, or special continuation command.

A small GitHub Action could eventually validate the human-result comment and change the label automatically, but that is optional. Manual state switching is already simple and transparent.

What a generic agent does next

Every generic agent invocation should use this priority order:

1. Open issues with state agent_ready
2. Open issues needing agent repair/finalization
3. Only then select a brand-new TaskGraph task

For an agent_ready issue, it reads:

Task ID
Current phase
Branch
Handoff commit
Latest valid workflow event
Human result
Human notes
Task contract identity
Checkout state

Then it creates an optimistic lease:

state_version: 6
state: agent_working
worker_id: task-review-agent-<unique-run>

It re-reads the issue to confirm its lease is still the latest transition. If another agent won the race, it stops and chooses another issue.

Then:

Human result was PASS
verify the human tested the recorded commit
        ↓
fetch/resume branch
        ↓
run authoritative validation
        ↓
package delivery evidence
        ↓
continue closeout/merge workflow
Human result was FAIL
verify the human tested the recorded commit
        ↓
resume the same branch
        ↓
use the human reproduction notes as repair feedback
        ↓
repair implementation
        ↓
commit and push a new commit
        ↓
write a new human checklist
        ↓
state returns to HUMAN_ACTION_REQUIRED

The loop can repeat as many times as needed while preserving the full history.

Important guardrails

The generic agent must stop rather than resume when:

the branch no longer exists;
the recorded commit cannot be found;
the branch has moved beyond the human-tested commit without a corresponding event;
the checkout is dirty in an unexplained way;
the issue label says agent_ready but no valid human-result event exists;
both PASS and FAIL are recorded for the same handoff;
the task contract changed;
the event chain is broken;
another agent already holds the current lease.

The issue tells the agent where to resume. Git and TaskGraph still tell it whether resumption is technically valid.

How this changes our current implementation

The current coordination layer only infers ownership from assignment plus the latest TaskReviewAgent claim comment.

We should replace that implicit model with:

IssueWorkflowState
WorkflowEvent
WorkflowEventChain
IssueStateLabel
HumanResult
AgentLease

The checkout manager can continue using its existing branch, commit, task-contract, remote, cleanliness, and external-manifest verification.

But its gate changes from:

coordination.status == claimed_by_worker

to something like:

state == agent_working
current_worker_id == this worker
phase permits checkout preparation
event chain is valid
Revised next development slice

The next work should no longer merely “add GitHub claim.”

It should implement the full operational issue state layer:

read managed issue state
        ↓
verify structured event log
        ↓
transition available/agent_ready → agent_working
        ↓
create or update Issue body dashboard
        ↓
append agent lease event
        ↓
perform checkout work
        ↓
eventually commit + push branch
        ↓
publish human task/checklist
        ↓
transition → human_action_required

Then the generic agent launcher searches for agent_ready issues before considering new TaskGraph work.

The issue becomes the resume token, the queue, the journal, and the human checklist. That is the workflow we should build.

What the Issue records

Every managed Issue has a visible dashboard showing:

Current state
Current owner
Current phase
Branch
Commit
Checkout
Next action

It also contains a hidden, machine-readable state object with the task ID, current owner, phase, agent lease, branch, commit, checkout, contract hash, human result, state version, and last event ID.

Every transition is recorded as an append-only Issue comment with a SHA-256 identity and a link to the previous event. The workflow rejects:

Missing or duplicate events
Reordered or edited events
Broken event chains
Wrong task or contract identity
State that disagrees with the final event
Multiple state blocks
State labels that disagree with the hidden state

The available operational states are:

State	Who acts next
agent_ready	Any generic agent may resume
agent_working	Only the agent holding the recorded lease
human_action_required	You
blocked	Human decision or external dependency
complete	Nobody

The phase separately tells the agent whether the next work is implementation, repair, Unity validation, delivery evidence, or merge closeout.

How generic agents resume work

Repository instructions now require a generic agent to check:

python Pipeline/TaskReviewAgent/issue_queue.py --source .

before selecting a fresh TaskGraph task.

That command returns only Issues where all three agree:

The hidden workflow state is agent_ready.
The complete event chain is valid.
The label is exactly nsc-state:agent-ready.

A generic agent must resume those Issues before selecting new not_delivered work. It uses the Issue’s phase, branch, commit, checkout, and human result rather than relying on previous chat context.

Agent leases and resource conflicts

Before beginning task work, the agent now:

Validates the task and its dependencies.
Checks managed Issues for overlapping exclusive resources.
Creates or initializes the Issue when needed.
Records its worker ID, source commit, planned branch, checkout, implementation approach, and validation plan.
Appends a hashed agent_lease_acquired event.
Changes the Issue to agent_working.
Re-reads the Issue and verifies the resulting event chain.

Tasks waiting for your Unity work continue reserving their scenes, prefabs, logical systems, and other exclusive resources. A second agent cannot begin conflicting work merely because the first agent has temporarily handed control to you.

Each PowerShell launch now receives a unique worker ID unless one is deliberately supplied, so separate agent windows do not accidentally share an identity.

What the human handoff will contain

The workflow refuses to hand a task to you until it proves:

The canonical task branch is checked out.
The working tree is completely clean.
The recorded commit is the checkout’s HEAD.
The commit descends from the workflow’s recorded base or previous handoff.
The exact commit is pushed as the remote task branch.
The checkout remote matches the controller repository.
The commit still contains the current task-contract identity.

The Issue handoff then includes:

Exact branch
Exact commit to test
Canonical checkout path
Concrete description of what was implemented
Checks the agent already completed
Numbered Unity steps for you
Expected behavior
PASS/FAIL result template

The Issue changes to:

human_action_required / unity_runtime_validation

At that point the agent stops.

3. Break the door.

You then add:

A GitHub Action validates that:

The Issue is actually waiting for you.
The event chain is intact.
The result says exactly PASS or FAIL.
The tested commit exactly matches the handoff commit.

Then it performs the transition:

PASS → agent_ready / delivery_evidence
FAIL → agent_ready / repair

A malformed result, missing result, or wrong commit is rejected. The Action restores nsc-state:human-action, explains the failure in a comment, and leaves the task assigned to you.

What the next generic agent does

After a PASS, the next agent resumes the recorded branch and commit and proceeds toward authoritative validation, delivery evidence, conformance, and closeout.

After a FAIL, the next agent resumes the same branch at the commit you tested, reads your failure report, repairs the behavior, commits and pushes a new commit, and creates a new human checklist.

The history remains in one Issue:

Agent lease
Implementation handoff
Human failure
Repair lease
Repair handoff
Human pass
Delivery lease
Closeout
Checkout continuity

The task checkout remains:

C:\UnityProjects\NoSafeCircleAgentCrew\<TASK-ID>

Its identity now belongs to the task and branch—not to the original agent worker. A later agent can:

Resume the existing exact clean checkout, or
Recreate it from the exact pushed task branch and handoff commit.

I also added coverage for main advancing while you are testing. An unrelated change to main does not rewrite or invalidate the human-tested task branch. The later agent continues from the commit you actually tested. Wrong branch, commit, remote, task hash, or dirty state still stops for reconciliation.

What was added
connected_pipeline.py

This is the deterministic implementation coordinator. It provides:

Bounded repository file listing, searching, and reading.
Exact implementation/test path validation.
Existing-versus-new path classification.
Task-owned resource-directory enforcement.
Test-directory enforcement.
Persisted hash-bound scope plans.
ExecutionCrew invocation and retry records.
Candidate SHA-256 verification.
Exact changed-path verification.
Patch applicability checks.
TaskGraph validation.
Unity batch-test execution.
Controlled rollback after failed tests.
Normal Git commit and non-force push.
Remote branch/commit verification.
Final durable Issue handoff.

The OpenAI agent does not directly receive shell, patch, commit, or push access.

execution_crew_bridge.py

This connects the new supervisor to the existing ExecutionCrew rather than replacing it.

It:

Runs inside claude-exec or codex-exec.
Uses the fixed Compose project name nosafecircle.
Introspects ExecutionCrew’s real argument parser.
Maps the validated path plan into its existing CLI.
Supports both initial runs and --retry-run with review feedback.
Finds and parses the resulting crew_result.json.
Returns the real run directory, status, candidate path, and candidate SHA-256.

This means we are finally using your existing crew as one stage in a larger goal loop rather than making you manually transfer its artifacts.

openai_pipeline.py

This is the goal-oriented OpenAI manager.

Its tools are limited to:

observe_goal_state
acquire_agent_lease
prepare_task_checkout
read_issue_log
repository_facts
list_repository_files
search_repository
read_repository_file
validate_execution_scope
run_execution_crew
retry_execution_crew
apply_test_commit_push_candidate
publish_human_handoff

It must re-observe state after each state-changing action.

For a repair phase, it reads your latest Issue comments and uses your reproduction details as repair input.

Deterministic path planning

Before ExecutionCrew receives any write authority, the agent must identify exact files in four lists:

{
  "existing_implementation_paths": [],
  "new_implementation_paths": [],
  "existing_test_paths": [],
  "new_test_paths": []
}

The validator rejects:

A missing file classified as existing.
An existing file classified as new.
Paths outside Assets/.
Direct .meta authority.
Tests outside an Assets/**/Tests/ directory.
Implementation files placed inside a test directory.
Implementation and test overlap.
New files whose parent is not a committed Git directory.
Implementation paths outside the task-owned resource directories.
A stale plan after branch, commit, or task-contract changes.

The model can correct a rejected plan, but it cannot bypass validation.

Candidate application is no longer a manual step

A candidate can be applied only when all of these are true:

crew_status == review_ready
candidate.patch exists
candidate SHA-256 matches
checkout is clean
checkout branch matches the validated plan
checkout HEAD matches the validated source
task contract identity matches
git apply --check --index passes
actual changed paths exactly equal approved paths

New Unity .meta files may accompany an approved new file, but the model cannot independently request arbitrary .meta write authority.

An unrelated changed file causes the entire candidate to be rejected.

Automated Unity validation

After applying the candidate—but before committing—the coordinator runs the Unity test platforms required by the task’s completion gates.

For example:

“Play Mode” in completion gates → PlayMode
“Edit Mode” in completion gates → EditMode
both mentioned → both run
neither specified → both run conservatively

Unity is resolved from:

The explicit -UnityExecutable argument.
UNITY_EXECUTABLE.
UNITY_PATH.
The Unity Hub installation matching ProjectVersion.txt.
A Unity executable on PATH.

If Unity cannot be found, the run stops. It does not silently commit uncompiled code.

On test failure

The pipeline:

Preserves XML and Unity logs outside committed gameplay files.
Creates concise ExecutionCrew feedback containing failed platform, exit code, mutation findings, and relevant log tail.
Restores the checkout to its exact clean pre-candidate commit.
Calls the existing ExecutionCrew retry mechanism.
Attempts the corrected review_ready candidate.

A failed candidate is never committed or pushed.

Commit and push behavior

When validation passes, the agent creates a normal commit such as:

Implement NSC-###: <Task Title>

It then performs a normal push:

local task branch
        ↓
origin/<same-task-branch>

There is no force push.

After pushing, it fetches the remote branch and proves:

local HEAD == origin task branch HEAD

The checkout must also be completely clean after the commit.

Your Issue handoff

Only after that pushed-commit proof passes does the agent update the Issue to:

human_action_required / unity_runtime_validation

The Issue contains:

Current state
Current owner
Current phase
Canonical checkout
Exact branch
Exact commit to test
What was implemented
Automated checks already completed
Numbered Unity runtime steps
Expected result
PASS/FAIL response template

The durable Issue remains the operational controller and event log. Its state, phase, branch, commit, and hash-linked events allow a later generic agent to continue without any previous chat context.

Your interaction remains:

Open the recorded checkout.
Verify the exact branch and commit shown in the Issue.
Perform the numbered Unity steps.
Post Result: PASS or Result: FAIL with the exact tested commit.
Add nsc-state:agent-ready.

The GitHub Action validates that result and routes:

PASS → agent_ready / delivery_evidence
FAIL → agent_ready / repair

A wrong commit, missing result, broken event chain, or stale state is rejected, and the Issue remains human-owned.

Command

After PR #59 is merged and your local main is updated, the connected explicit-task command is:

powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File .\Pipeline\TaskReviewAgent\Start-TaskReviewAgent.ps1 `
  -TaskId NSC-### `
  -Mode openai-pipeline-real `
  -ExecutionProvider claude

With an explicit Unity executable:

powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File .\Pipeline\TaskReviewAgent\Start-TaskReviewAgent.ps1 `
  -TaskId NSC-### `
  -Mode openai-pipeline-real `
  -ExecutionProvider claude `
  -UnityExecutable "C:\Program Files\Unity\Hub\Editor\<UNITY-VERSION>\Editor\Unity.exe"

Required environment:

$env:OPENAI_API_KEY = "..."

Provider authentication must also already be available to the selected Docker service.

The default goal-loop budget was raised from 24 to 80 turns because this run now includes repository investigation, planning, implementation, retries, testing, Git delivery, and Issue handoff.

Generic-agent continuation

No special task-resume command was introduced.

Repository guidance still requires every generic agent to begin with:

python Pipeline/TaskReviewAgent/issue_queue.py --source .

It resumes a validated agent_ready Issue before considering fresh TaskGraph work.

For a human failure, the Issue phase is repair, and the connected agent reads your failure comment before changing code.

For a human pass, the Issue phase is delivery_evidence, so a generic agent knows implementation is finished and should move downstream instead of running ExecutionCrew again.

The downstream workflow is connected

The work is complete on draft PR #59, now titled “Pipeline: durable task workflow through verified merge closeout.” The pull request remains open and draft; main has not been changed.

The same generic command now determines what to do from the durable Issue state:

powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File .\Pipeline\TaskReviewAgent\Start-GameTaskAgent.ps1

There is still no task-specific restart command.

Complete workflow
Generic agent starts
        ↓
Resume validated agent-ready Issue before selecting new work
        ↓
Acquire agent lease
        ↓
Create or resume canonical task checkout
        ↓
Plan exact implementation and test paths
        ↓
Run ExecutionCrew
        ↓
Apply validated candidate
        ↓
Run automated Unity validation and bounded repair

        ↓
Vincent records PASS or FAIL
        ↓
PASS → DELIVERY_EVIDENCE
FAIL → REPAIR
        ↓
Run authoritative clean Unity validation manifests

HUMAN DELIVERY-EVIDENCE REVIEW
